### Page 7

7

The 
 multiple 
 origins of chronic investment shortage  
Households are 
 risk
 -
averse.  
 Germany’s high level of saving is usually attributed to prudent, highly risk
 -
averse 
households. Indeed, German households’ saving rate out of disposable income (Figure 7) is high in international 
comparison. At the same time, it is “natural” for the house
 hold sector in aggregate to feature a surplus of saving 
over investment. Households face an uncertain income stream but wish to smooth consumption. Consequently, they 
spread resources over a lifetime through saving  
making household surpluses a common feature of most economies.

Figure 7: Household gross saving in % of disposable income (2025): German households are heavy savers.

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research 
Together with other factors such as risk aversion, the lack of a “Kapitalmarktkultur” (“capital markets culture”) and 
financial literacy
 7
, the institutional setup of pensions provision affects household saving incentives substantially: The 
public pay
 -
as
 -
you
-
go pension system reduces the need for households to bear market risk for retirement provision. 
As a result, German households hold 37%  
of their financial assets in bank deposits (Figure 8).  
Figure 8: Asset classes as % of total gross financial assets: Bank deposits still a key saving vehicle

Sources: Eurostat, national central banks, financial supervisory authorities, financial associations and statistical 
offices, IMF, LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research. 
In turn, banks intermediate those savings in bank deposits into low
 -
risk, low
 -
return assets: corporate loans, which 
favor incumbent firms (due to established relationships) rather than new, innovative firms; and government bonds 
(due to favorable regulator
 y treatment of banks’ holdings). As a result, savings contribute little to the creation of 
risk
-
bearing capital, and available funds for startups and scaling is scarce.

Figure 5 suggests that, in addition to households, two more sectors have in recent years tended to have a surplus of 
saving over investment: the general government 
 (
federal, state, and local government together with social security 
funds
 )
, and non
 -

The lack of government investment contributed to a lack of demand and poor infrastructure. 
 Government 
surpluses of saving over investment partly reflected a lack of public investment. Between 2002 and 2015 net 
government investment (gross investment less depreciation on the public capital stock) was zero, on average 
(Figure 9). The government ba
 rely invested enough to keep the public capital stock from shrinking.  
Only since 2015 gross investment has been sufficient to keep net investment positive. At 3.5% of GDP in 2025, 
government gross investment  
is now catching up with peers. As a result of this lack of government investment, 
Germany’s infrastructure has deteriorated, which is likely to have contributed to the lack of domestic investment by 
firms.
8  
Figure 9: General government gross vs net investment in % of GDP: Improvement underway

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research 
Corporate surpluses persist.  
Figure 5 shows that the non
 -
financial corporate (NFC) sector has exhibited a surplus 
of saving over investment since around the turn of the millennium. Corporate saving is  
simply retained earnings, i.e. 
profits kept in the firm after paying interest, taxes and dividends. These surpluses became substantial after the great 
financial crisis and have persisted since at macro
 -
significant levels (on average since 2010: 1.6 % of G
 DP; 2025 still 
1.1% of GDP). While an excess of saving over investment is “natural” for households, it is not necessarily the case for 
NFCs. Firms invest to earn  
returns and will usually borrow to invest (or issue equity). Figure 10 shows the persistent 
shortfall of NFC investment vs saving.  
Figure 10: German non-financial corporations, gross saving vs gross investment, % of GDP: The gap persists

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research

There are several reasons for the NFC surplus.  
• 
 Wage moderation and strong external demand
 . Real wages in the NFC sector have lagged productivity 
for nearly two decades (Figure 11). Only recently has real compensation for employees caught up. All else 
equal, wages lagging productivity would have boosted firms’ profits. Strong external demand, i
 f not 
accompanied by a commensurate increase in investment spending, will do the same. As shown in Figure 
1
0
, gross NFC saving jumped during periods of strong global demand (2009
 -
11 and after the pandemic, 
2020
 -
22). 
• 
 Lack of domestic demand
 . Wage moderation and a lack of public investment contributed to sluggish 
domestic demand, in turn suppressing economic growth. One consequence was that it made investment in 
faster
 -
growing overseas markets more attractive.  
• 
 Tax incentives favoring retained earnings.  
German corporate tax reforms (2001, 2008) made retaining 
profits more attractive than paying dividends, lowering payout rates and raising NFC gross saving.
 9 
These 
incentives matter in Germany because a large share of corporate profits are generated by privately held 
firms. In privately held firms, owners’ personal tax incentives, rather than pure profit maximization, may 
shape payout decisions. As the additio
 nal saving was not absorbed by investment (Figure 10), it resulted in 
higher net lending.  
Figure 11: Real compensation vs real value added per hour in the non -financial corporate sector (index, 2000 = 
100): wage restraint has contributed to non-financial sector surpluses until recently

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research 
Economic theory suggests that years of underinvestment should raise expected returns and attract capital. In 
Germany, however, a range of supply
 -
side constraints is preventing this adjustment from taking place. 
 Energy 
costs remain elevated following the loss of cheap Russian pipeline gas and the shift towards more expensive LNG 
imports, compounded by high taxes and network charges. Businesses surveyed by EY in 2026 also see the cost of 
energy as Europe’s main dis
 advantage as an investment location, although 60% of them expect Europe’s 
attractiveness to improve over the next three years.
 10 
Labor costs are also burdened by Germany's exceptionally 
high tax wedge, the second highest in the OECD. Bureaucracy further weighs on competitiveness. According to the 
ifo economic research institute, excessive administrative requirements reduce annual o
 utput by up to EUR 146bn, 
or roughly 4% of GDP.  
11 
Although Germany ranks 12th out of 45 countries for overall business attractiveness, it ranks 
second
 -
to
-
last on costs.  
12 
Other structural weaknesses are equally apparent. Demographic decline is reducing the 
supply of skilled labor, while transport, logistics and digital infrastructure bear the consequences of years of 
underinvestment. Financing conditions are less favorable  
than in France or Italy, despite lower German risk
 -
free 
rates, with the fragmented banking system likely playing a role. These long
 -
standing challenges are now being 
compounded by structural shocks. Competition from China has intensified to the point that  
Germany runs a negative 
trade balance with China even in capital goods, for decades its core strength. Meanwhile, parts of the automotive 
sector misjudged both technology and market positioning, favoring combustion engines and fuel cell technology

over affordable electric vehicles. As a result, the higher returns that theory predicts after prolonged underinvestment 
are being eroded by a combination of cost pressures, institutional inefficiencies and structural headwinds.

What needs to be done: Germany’s fiscal push is a first step, but private capital needs to 
follow  
Germany’s fiscal push heralds potential investment turnaround. 
 Yet at least some of the demand
 -
side reasons for 
Germany’s large surpluses of saving over investment are on the mend. The German government has put in place a 
large fiscal expansion centered on infrastructure capex and defense. 
 Following the reform of its debt brake in 2025, 
public deficits are expected to rise from around 2
 -
3% to 4
-
5% of GDP in the next years, funding hundreds of billions 
of euros in additional spending on infrastructure, defense, energy, climate mitigation & ad
 aption and digitalization
 . 
The government’s fiscal package thus promises to kill two birds with one stone when it comes to improving German 
investment perspectives. In the short run it boosts investment spending, while in the longer run it improves public 
infrastructure, spurring 
 private investment demand.  
Government money alone is not enough: attracting private capital is necessary.  
Closing Germany’s investment 
gap will require more than public funding. The scale of the country’s investment needs is such that substantial 
private capital must also be mobilized. Foreign capital is already responding to some extent. 
 B
ased on Bundesbank 
data, FDI inflows rose by about 50% to around EUR86bn in 2025, after a fall of almost 32% in 2024  
(Figure 1
 2
)
.
13 
Investors from the UK raised their investment by 284% to EUR26bn and are now the largest single source (31% of 
the total), while U.S. investment fell by 44% to EUR11.8bn. Other EU countries contributed EUR43bn, so European 
investors account for more than 
 80% of the total.  
Figure 12: Foreign direct investment inflows to Germany, EUR bn and % of GDP: Rebound in the making

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research 
Germany's challenge is not a shortage of capital but rather directing this capital towards productive investment.  
German households hold around EUR9trn in financial assets, a large proportion of which, by international 
standards, sits in cash and bank deposits (EUR3.4trn).
 14
. Meanwhile, the country's net foreign asset position exceeds 
EUR4.0trn (87% of GDP).  Unlocking even a small portion of these savings could have a transformative impact. 
Shifting just 5% of German households' financial assets out of deposits into more pro
 ductive assets would mobilize 
roughly EUR450bn of investment capital. In addition, repatriating for example 10% of the country’s net foreign 
investments would translate to EUR400bn of additional domestic capital.  
Making Germany more attractive for private investment starts with raising expected returns.  
 Years of 
underinvestment have already increased the potential returns on capital, as scarcity raises the marginal product of 
capital. But unlocking this opportunity requires reducing regulatory barriers, expanding standardized investment 
structures and st
 rengthening the investment culture through tax incentives, pension and insurance reforms, public
 -
private partnerships standards and strategic investment funds. Bottlenecks such as permitting, procurement, deal 
structuring and regulatory friction must be re
 duced. A key enabler for all relevant investment opportunities is

times. The rapid build
 -

quick infrastructure investments.  
The newly set up “Deutschlandfonds,” with its EUR30bn guarantees and equity targeting to mobilize EUR130bn 
in private capital, is an important step in the right direction  
as it directly targets the gap between Germany's

“Water, water, everywhere, nor any drop to drink”: Germany’s intermediation problem.  
The issue of available 
saving versus domestic investment 
 – 
or the country’s current account surplus 
 – 
is in large part about 
 where  
Germany 
invests. A related, but separate, question is how the savings that stay domestic are used. It turns out that another 
factor holding back investment in Germany is the intermediation of existing savings. Germany, like many other EU 
countries, is par
 ticularly lacking in long
 -
term capital due to the underdevelopment of pension funds (Figure 1
 3
), 
since public pay
 -
as
 -
you
-

 15 
Key reform: towards a more funded retirement system
 . Scandinavia and the Netherlands, economies with similar 
“culture” to Germany, have much higher stock market capitalization as a share of GDP not least because households 
accumulate claims on private pensions, which in turn recycle savings into the domest
 ic equity market (Figure 1
 4
).
16 
The development of pension funds is the key reform because they are upstream of venture capital and private 
equity in the financing chain.  
Figure 13: Insurance and pensions assets as % of nominal GDP, 2025: underdeveloped in Germany

Sources: Eurostat, national central banks, financial supervisory authorities, financial associations and statistical 
offices, IMF, LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research. 
The pension commission’s proposal for a funded “Kapitalrente” financed by an additional 2% contribution 
starting 
 from 2028 and invested in a public fund alongside private alternatives, would be a major step in the 
right direction
 .
17 
It would add a capital
 -
funded component to the pay
 -
as
 -
you
-
go system in pillar 1 and channel 
household retirement saving into equity markets at scale. According to estimates, the public fund could reach 
around EUR560bn by 2040 (8% of GDP) and become one of  
the world’s largest pension funds within two decades.
 18 
This would create a domestic institutional investor base that sits upstream from venture capital and private equity.  
As an additional pillar 3 contribution to Germany's pension system, the forthcoming “Altersvorsorgedepot” can 
also strengthen domestic investment.  
This reform, which has already been passed, will replace the Riester pension 
from 1 January 2027. Savers will be able to invest in shares, funds and ETFs without a capital guarantee
 . 
The state 
will pay up to 50 cents for every euro saved, up to a total of 360 euros, and an additional 25 cents for every euro 
saved up to a total of 1,800 euros. This results in a maximum annual subsidy of 540 euros.

How to invest in Germany: focus on key investment themes and incentives  
From underinvestment to investment opportunity 
 – 
why now is the right time to close Germany’s up to EUR730bn 
investment gap.  
Germany's investment gap presents both a problem and an opportunity. Years of underinvestment 
have created enormous modernization needs and, in principle, high marginal returns to new capital. Until now, 
structural bottlenecks prevented investors from cap
 turing those returns. The combination of fiscal expansion, reform 
and public
 -
private risk sharing could now begin to change that equation.  The key question that arises next is where 
and how to invest. The answer can be found in four key areas: energy, dig
 ital infrastructure, transport and mobility 
and defense.  
Energy transition:  
The move towards net
 -
zero emissions and greater geopolitical resilience will require substantial 
investment in renewable energy, electricity grids, storage solutions and hydrogen infrastructure. Germany will 
require around EUR1.3tn in investments for rene
 wables, grid infrastructure, storage and hydrogen by 2045, which 
is equivalent to 30% of GDP in 2025.  Financing vehicles and project pipelines are key enablers. However, with 
around 11,000 local authorities and 800 municipal utilities (“Stadtwerke”), indi
 vidual projects are often too small to 
attract institutional investors. To address this, municipalities and regions should be supported through advisory 
services, standardized tender frameworks and training programs to facilitate the structuring of investa
 ble public
 -
private partnerships. Greater participation by districts (“
 Regierungsbezirke”) and counties (“Kreise”) would facilitate 
project aggregation and create scalable investment pipelines. Foreign investors are showing interest here: 
according to EY, the number of low
 -
carbon energy FDI projects in Europe rose by 25% in 2
 025.
21 
Digital infrastructure:  
 Digitalization and digital infrastructure are critical drivers of productivity growth. It is 
estimated that a cumulative investment of between EUR275bn and 500bn will be required by 2030, equivalent to 
6
–
11% of GDP in 2025. This includes spending on compu
 ter hardware, telecommunications equipment, software 
and AI
 -
related infrastructure, such as data centers. Grid access and permitting reform are key enablers. Germany 
should therefore prioritize grid
 -
connection approvals and permitting procedures for data c
 enters and digital 
infrastructure, treating computing capacity with the same urgency as energy security. Multi
 -
year connection queues 
have become the primary constraint on Germany’s AI and digital expansion, rather than a lack of capital or demand. 
Foreign  
capital is already 
 committing: Google plans to invest EUR5.5bn in Germany until 2029, Microsoft announced

19 
Indefi (2026): The German Pension Reform and the Shape of Things to Come  
20 
In addition, the “Frühstartrente” would extend capital
 -
funded provision to children: the state pays 
 EUR
 10 monthly into a depot from age six 
until 18. 
However, the resulting macroeconomic investment volume would be lower than that of the other additional reforms. The Bundesta
 g is 
set to vote on this measure on 16 October 2026.

EY (2026), European Attractiveness Survey, June 2026. The figures for low

EUR3.2bn in 2024 and Nvidia is part of a EUR1bn data center project with Deutsche Telekom in Munich.
 22 
AI
-
related 
FDI projects in Europe rose by 96% in 2025, and the number also went up in Germany although total FDI projects 
declined.  
Transport and mobility: 
 Modernizing transport infrastructure is another major investment priority. The Ministry of 
Transport plans to invest EUR166bn between 2025 and 2029, including EUR107bn for rail, EUR52bn for federal 
roads, and EUR8bn for waterways. A key enabler is performa
 nce
-
based concessions. A new generation of public
 -
private partnerships (PPP 2.0) should be based on performance
 -
linked service
 -
level agreements and long
 -
term 
offtake contracts that link investor returns to measurable and predictable cash flows.  
Defense: 
 German defense financing is expected to reach approximately EUR734bn over five years, enabling the 
country to meet NATO's target of spending 3.5% of GDP on core defense by 2029. Planned procurement includes 
land systems, air power, air and missile defense,  
long
 -
range strike capabilities, unmanned systems, munitions, and 
space security assets. Foreign investors are also moving in: the number of defense FDI projects in Europe rose by 
84% in 2025 to 107.
 23 
Only seven of them were in Germany, against 20 in the UK, 14 in France, 12 in Ukraine, 10 in 
Spain and nine in Poland (Figure 14).  Key enabler: simplification. As in other sectors, reducing regulatory and 
procurement complexity will be crucial to acceler
 ating investment and delivery.

What is at stake  
Germany has a rare opportunity to transform today's fiscal stimulus into a sustained investment boom. 
 If 
successful, the country could benefit significantly from a renewed capital influx. Not only would it lift growth from 
the demand side in the short run, it would also raise potential growth and boost long
 -
term growth for decades. The 
benefits would exten
 d beyond Germany 
 – 
Europe cannot afford for its largest economy to remain its weakest link.  
The gains would be economic and political. 
 Stronger growth, functioning infrastructure and rising prosperity would 
help to restore confidence in institutions, strengthening the foundations of the social state and reducing some of the 
economic drivers behind the current political polarization. Howev
 er, successful and bold reforms, as well as 
investors willing to support Germany's renewed momentum, are equally essential to achieving this outcome.  
Failure would come at a high cost. 
 While fiscal spending could provide a temporary boost, structural weaknesses 
would persist, leaving Germany with higher debt and little improvement in long
 -
term growth. The consequences 
would extend beyond the economic sphere, including continued dependenc
 e on foreign energy and technology, 
further erosion of Europe's stability and competitiveness, and continued political polarization and populism. 
Germany's investment challenge is therefore about much more than just growth. It concerns the future economic 
and political resilience of Germany and Europe as a whole.

Điểm nhấn thị trường 
Chỉ số, nhóm ngành, khối ngoại và phái sinh

Top 5 ngành tăng / giảm  VNINDEX tăng điểm trong tuần qua, 20.45 điểm tương ứng 1.14%, với thanh khoản cải 
thiện. Dầu khí, Bảo hiểm và Tiện ích khí là các nhóm ngành có mức tăng rõ nét nhất trong 
tuần. Khối ngoại đảo chiều mua ròng so với xu hướng của tuần trước với giá trị 2,680 tỷ 
đồng, tập trung ở các mã FPT, HDB và BSR; đồng thời, tự doanh đảo chiều mua ròng ở 
mức 1,172 tỷ đồng.

Ở một khía cạnh khác, khối ngoại mua ròng 3,009 HĐTL VN30F1M trong tuần qua và đang 
duy trì vị thế mua lũy kế 5,001 hợp đồng. Tự doanh bán ròng -9,259 hợp đồng và đang duy 
trì trạng thái lũy kế bán ròng 9,458 hợp đồng.

TTCK Việt Nam có tuần tăng điểm tích cực, chỉ số VNINDEX chinh phục thành công 
ngưỡng tâm lý 1.800 điểm với thanh khoản cải thiện, độ rộng thị trường nghiêng về số mã 
tăng điểm và nhà đầu tư nước ngoài quay trở lại mua ròng. Động lực hỗ trợ thị trường 
trong tuần qua đến từ hoạt động cơ cấu của các quỹ bám theo rổ chỉ số mới nổi của FTSE, 
khi mà ngày 21/9 là ngày Việt Nam được chính thức nâng hạng. Thêm vào đó, diễn biến hạ 
nhiệt của giá dầu khi lo ngại thiếu hụt cung do xung đột ở Trung Đông giảm bớt cũng là 
yếu tố hỗ trợ tâm lý thị trường chung. Ở chiều ngược lại, thông tin FED tăng lãi suất có tác 
động tiêu cực nhưng ảnh hưởng không đáng kể lên thị trường do động thái này đã được 
dự báo từ trước.

### Page 3

Fed tăng lãi suất, dự báo thêm 
một lần tăng nữa trong năm 
Đánh giá áp lực lên tỷ giá và lãi suất Việt Nam

Fed tăng lãi suất và dự báo có thêm một lần tăng lãi suất nữa trong năm 
— Trong kỳ họp FOMC ngày 15–16/09/2026, Fed quyết định tăng 25bps lãi suất điều hành 
lên mức 3.75%–4%, đánh dấu lần tăng lãi suất đầu tiên kể từ năm 2023. Quyết định 
được thông qua với tỷ lệ đồng thuận 12–0, phản ánh sự thống nhất của các thành viên 
FOMC trong việc ưu tiên kiểm soát lạm phát. 
— Chủ tịch Fed Kevin Warsh nhấn mạnh lạm phát vẫn ở mức cao và cần được đưa về mục 
tiêu 2% trong thời gian phù hợp hơn. Bên cạnh đó, Fed đánh giá hoạt động kinh tế 
đang tăng trưởng vững, tiêu dùng nội địa duy trì tích cực, năng suất và đầu tư vốn tăng 
trưởng mạnh, trong khi thị trường lao động vẫn ổn định. Điều này cho thấy Fed chưa 
nhận thấy áp lực suy giảm kinh tế đủ lớn để ưu tiên nới lỏng chính sách tiền tệ.  Ông 
Warsh không đưa ra cam kết cụ thể về thời điểm tăng lãi suất, nhấn mạnh chính sách 
sẽ phụ thuộc vào diễn biến lạm phát, thị trường lao động và các dữ liệu kinh tế sắp tới. 
— Biểu đồ dot plot tháng 9 cho thấy Fed vẫn nghiêng về khả năng tăng thêm lãi suất 
trong năm 2026, với 16/18 thành viên dự báo ít nhất một lần tăng nữa, đưa lãi suất điều 
hành lên vùng 4%–4.25%. Tuy nhiên, sự phân hóa trong dự báo lãi suất năm 2027 
(3.625%–4.375%) cho thấy đây có thể là một lần điều chỉnh nhằm kiểm soát lạm phát, 
thay vì tín hiệu về một chu kỳ thắt chặt kéo dài.

Quan điểm của KBSV đối với lãi suất và tỷ giá 
— Trong 3 tháng cuối năm, chúng tôi đánh giá nhiều khả năng Fed sẽ có thêm một lần 
tăng lãi suất 25bps, dựa trên:  
(1) Fed tiếp tục nâng dự báo PCE headline và core 2026 lên thêm 10 bps so với dự 
báo hồi tháng 6, đồng thời dự báo lạm phát sẽ quay về mức mục tiêu 2% vào năm 
2029 (thay vì năm 2028 như dự báo trước đó), trong khi dự báo về tỷ lệ thất 
nghiệp và tăng trưởng GDP được điều chỉnh theo hướng tích cực hơn. 
(2) Giá dầu vẫn duy trì ở mức cao (dự kiến quay về vùng 9x USD/thùng vào quý 4) 
trong bối cảnh xung đột tại Trung Đông tiếp tục gây gián đoạn hoạt động vận 
chuyển và xuất khẩu dầu mỏ. Mặc dù Saudi Arabia đã tăng cường vận chuyển 
dầu qua Oman, qua đó giảm bớt lo ngại về thiếu hụt nguồn cung trong ngắn hạn, 
rủi ro gián đoạn vẫn hiện hữu khi xung đột chưa được giải quyết hoàn toàn. 
— Chúng tôi nâng dự báo DXY lên vùng 98–103 điểm trong phần còn lại của năm với động 
lực hỗ trợ USD trong ngắn hạn đến từ việc Fed phát tín hiệu tiếp tục thắt chặt, trong khi 
thị trường điều chỉnh kỳ vọng lãi suất theo hướng duy trì cao hơn. Tuy nhiên, việc các 
NHTW lớn khác cũng duy trì định hướng kiểm soát lạm phát có thể hạn chế mức độ mở 
rộng chênh lệch lãi suất, qua đó làm giảm khả năng USD tăng giá một chiều và DXY 
tăng mạnh khỏi vùng dự báo.

F I R S T  T O  M A R K E T

Kết quả kinh doanh 2Q2026 tích cực 
nhờ hưởng lợi từ căng thẳng địa 
chính trị ở Trung Đông

Giá dầu duy trì ở mức cao, tiếp tục hỗ 
trợ biên lợi nhuận khí khô của GAS

Luật Dầu khí sửa đổi mở rộng dư địa

tăng nguồn khí cho GAS trong dài 
hạn

Khuyến nghị MUA với giá mục tiêu 
96,300 VND/cổ phiếu

Doanh thu Q2/2026 tăng trưởng tích cực 44% YoY, chủ yếu nhờ (1) giá bán khí tăng 
và (2) tăng trưởng sản lượng tích cực từ mảng LNG. Trong khi đó, LNST-CĐTS tăng 
chậm hơn, ở mức 23% YoY, do ghi nhận khoản hoàn nhập dự phòng nợ phải thu khó 
đòi thấp hơn so với cùng kỳ năm trước.

Giá dầu Brent tăng trở lại lên vùng 95 USD/thùng trong bối cảnh xung đột Mỹ - Iran 
tiếp tục leo thang, làm gián đoạn nguồn cung dầu tại Trung Đông, trong khi lưu 
lượng tàu qua Eo biển Hormuz vẫn ở mức thấp và tồn kho dầu toàn cầu cùng Kho 
Dự trữ Chiến lược Mỹ liên tục suy giảm. Chúng tôi kỳ vọng giá dầu duy trì vùng 85 - 
95 USD/thùng trong ngắn hạn, qua đó tiếp tục hỗ trợ biên lợi nhuận mảng khí 
thượng nguồn của GAS nhờ chênh lệch giữa giá bán khí theo giá dầu và giá miệng 
giếng cố định tại các mỏ lâu đời.

Luật Dầu khí sửa đổi tăng ưu đãi cho các mỏ cận biên, giúp cải thiện hiệu quả đầu 
tư và thúc đẩy phát triển nguồn khí mới. GAS có thể hưởng lợi từ sản lượng khí bổ 
sung qua hệ thống hạ tầng hiện hữu, nhưng tác động chủ yếu trong trung và dài 
hạn.

Kết hợp 2 phương pháp FCFF và EV/EBITDA để định giá cổ phiếu GAS, chúng tôi đưa 
ra khuyến nghị MUA với mức giá mục tiêu 96,300 VND/cổ phiếu, tương ứng mức 
upside 16.2% so với giá đóng cửa ngày 11/09/2026.

Kết quả kinh doanh tích cực trong 
2Q2026

Giá urea hạ nhiệt, xuất khẩu tiếp tục 
là động lực dẫn dắt doanh thu chính 
của DCM trong 2H2026

Giá khí đầu vào DCM 2028–2030F 
tăng do bổ sung nguồn khí giá cao từ 
Nam Du – U Minh

Luật Dầu khí mới cải thiện triển vọng 
nguồn khí đầu vào cho DCM trong 
dài hạn

Khuyến nghị MUA với giá mục tiêu 
42,100 VND/cổ phiếu

Doanh thu Q2/2026 tăng 10% YoY, chủ yếu nhờ giá bán urea tăng trong bối cảnh thị 
trường chịu tác động từ các yếu tố địa chính trị. LNST-CĐTS tăng 36% YoY, được hỗ 
trợ bởi giá bán đầu ra tăng nhanh hơn chi phí khí đầu vào.

Giá urea thế giới sau khi tăng cao trong Q2/2026 đã điều chỉnh về vùng trước chiến 
tranh do nhu cầu suy yếu và nguồn cung Trung Quốc quay trở lại. Đối với DCM, xuất 
khẩu urea tiếp tục là động lực dẫn dắt doanh thu chính, trong khi giá và sản lượng 
urea nội địa được kỳ vọng đi ngang trong 2H2026 do đây không phải mùa cao điểm 
tiêu thụ trong nước.

Chúng tôi điều chỉnh tăng 10% dự báo giá khí đầu vào DCM giai đoạn 2028–2030F, 
phản ánh việc bổ sung nguồn khí Nam Du – U Minh từ Q4/2028. Giá khí Nam Du – U 
Minh dự kiến 11.56 USD/mmBTU, cao hơn ~35% so với các nguồn hiện hữu, tạo áp 
lực tăng chi phí đầu vào.

Luật Dầu khí sửa đổi sẽ rút ngắn quy trình phê duyệt FDP và bổ sung ưu đãi cho các 
mỏ khó khai thác, qua đó hỗ trợ đầu tư E&P và duy trì sản lượng dầu khí trong 
nước. Đối với DCM, tác động dự kiến mang tính gián tiếp và có độ trễ, chủ yếu thông 
qua cải thiện nguồn cung khí nội địa, chưa phải động lực thúc đẩy lợi nhuận đáng 
kể trong ngắn hạn.

Kết hợp 2 phương pháp FCFF và EV/EBITDA định giá cổ phiếu DCM chúng tôi đưa ra 
khuyến nghị MUA với mức giá mục tiêu 42,100 VND/cổ phiếu, tương ứng mức upside 
29.5% so với giá đóng cửa ngày 11/09/2026.

MUA duy trì 
Giá mục tiêu VND42,100 
Tổng mức sinh lời (%) 29.5% 
Giá hiện tại (11/09/2026) VND32,500

Doanh thu và LNST-CĐTS 2Q2026 
tiếp tục tăng trưởng tốt, được hỗ trợ 
bởi đà tăng của crack spread

Biên lợi nhuận duy trì ở mức cao 
trong ngắn hạn nhờ crack spread 
bùng nổ, nhưng có thể biến động 
mạnh

Biên lợi nhuận trung và dài hạn chịu 
áp lực từ rủi ro dư cung dầu

Khuyến nghị MUA với giá mục tiêu 
32,200 VND/cổ phiếu 
 Trong Q2/2026, doanh thu BSR tăng 60% YoY nhờ giá nhiên liệu tăng mạnh trong 
bối cảnh nguồn cung gián đoạn do xung đột Trung Đông. LNST-CĐTS tăng 807% 
YoY, được hỗ trợ bởi crack spread của bốn sản phẩm lọc dầu chủ lực tăng mạnh do 
nguồn cung thắt chặt.

Xung đột quân sự Mỹ – Iran gây gián đoạn nghiêm trọng dòng chảy dầu qua Eo biển 
Hormuz, thắt chặt nguồn cung dầu thô và thành phẩm, đẩy giá Brent lên quanh 100 
USD/thùng và đưa crack spread tại châu Á đạt đỉnh và duy trì ở vùng cao từ tháng 3 
đến nay. Diễn biến crack spread thời gian tới sẽ phụ thuộc đáng kể vào diễn biến 
chiến sự tại Eo biển Hormuz và tốc độ khôi phục lưu thông qua Eo biển này.

Triển vọng lợi nhuận trung và dài hạn của BSR chịu áp lực khi cán cân cung – cầu 
dầu toàn cầu được dự báo chuyển sang dư cung trong trung và dài hạn. Tuy nhiên, 
nhu cầu tái tích lũy tồn kho dầu toàn cầu có thể phần nào hấp thụ lượng cung dư 
thừa và hạn chế mức độ thu hẹp crack spread.

Kết hợp 2 phương pháp FCFF và EV/EBITDA định giá cổ phiếu BSR chúng tôi đưa ra 
khuyến nghị Mua với mức giá mục tiêu 32,200 VND/cổ phiếu, tương ứng mức upside 
16.9% so với giá đóng cửa ngày 11/09/2026. Chúng tôi áp dụng mức EV/EBITDA mục 
tiêu là 15.8 lần, bằng với mức chuẩn trung bình 5 năm của cổ phiếu.

BÁO CÁO NGẮN CƠ HỘI ĐẦU TƯ

15/09/2026

CTCP Nhựa Tiền Phong (NTP) 
Tăng tốc trong chu kỳ thuận lợi

Mua 
Giá mục tiêu VND 73.200 
Vùng hỗ trợ/Điểm mua 1 50.000 – 51.400 
Lợi nhuận kỳ vọng 1 44% 
Vùng hỗ trợ/Điểm mua 2 42.000 – 44.000 
Lợi nhuận kỳ vọng 2 70%

Lưu ý: 
Nhà đầu tư cần kết hợp nhận định xu hướng thị 
trường chung, mức độ hấp dẫn của các điểm 
nhấn đầu tư và khẩu vị rủi ro của mình để quản 
lý tỷ trọng tại từng điểm mua cho phù hợp. 
Do việc xác định mỗi điểm mua được dựa trên 
các khung thời gian khác nhau nên chúng tôi 
không đưa ra mức dừng lỗ cố định. Nhìn chung, 
mức dừng lỗ nên được đặt chặt hơn cho điểm 
mua gần (5-7%) và rộng hơn cho điểm mua xa 
(10-15%).

ĐIỂM NHẤN ĐẦU TƯ 
Kỳ vọng sản lượng tiêu thụ tăng trưởng mạnh hơn so với giai đoạn trước. KBSV 
dự báo sản lượng tiêu thụ của NTP tăng 2,3% CAGR giai đoạn 2026 - 2028 nhờ (1) 
Đẩy mạnh giải ngân đầu tư hạ tầng tại miền Bắc (2) gia tăng đầu tư hạ tầng tại các 
địa phương thu hút FDI (3) tăng tốc đơn hàng mảng dự án.

Giá hạt nhựa sẽ gây áp lực biên lợi nhuận nửa cuối 2026 nhưng không làm thay 
đổi triển vọng dài hạn. Mặc dù duy trì chính sách dự phòng nguyên liệu cao và đa 
dạng hóa nhà cung cấp, nhưng NTP vẫn đối mặt với rủi ro thu hẹp lợi nhuận, 
nhưng sẽ không làm giảm đáng kể triển vọng tăng trưởng trung hạn. KBSV cho 
rằng tình trạng dư cung hạt nhựa tại Trung Quốc vẫn sẽ duy trì trong trung hạn và 
xung đột được giải quyết, giảm áp lực biên lợi nhuận toàn ngành.

Catalyst thoái vốn kỳ vọng tái định giá doanh nghiệp. Theo kế hoạch thoái vốn 
Nhà nước, SCIC dự kiến thoái toàn bộ 37,1% cổ phần tại NTP . Chúng tôi đánh giá 
việc hiện thực hóa thương vụ có thể giúp NTP (1) thu hút nhà đầu tư chiến lược 
mới; (2) cộng hưởng mô hình kinh doanh; (3) tái định giá doanh nghiệp.

Hoạt động kinh doanh  Diễn biến doanh thu và lợi nhuận thuần 5 năm gần nhất

NTP là một trong những doanh nghiệp đầu 
ngành ống nhựa tại Việt Nam, với thị phần 
khoảng 31% toàn quốc và 60% tại miền Bắc. 
Hoạt động kinh doanh cốt lõi tập trung vào 
sản xuất và phân phối ống nhựa cùng các sản 
phẩm phụ tùng. Nhờ lợi thế về thương hiệu, 
quy mô và mạng lưới phân phối, NTP duy trì 
vị thế dẫn đầu và nền tảng tăng trưởng ổn 
định qua các năm.

Nguồn: Báo cáo công ty, KB Securities Vietnam

Tương quan mặt bằng giá  Diễn biến P/E của doanh nghiệp

NTP đang được giao dịch với mức P/E khoảng 
8,3 lần thấp hơn trung bình 5 năm của cổ 
phiếu. Với kỳ vọng doanh nghiệp sẽ bước vào 
pha tăng trưởng mạnh trong 3 năm tới, chúng 
tôi cho rằng P/E fw 2026F ở mức 9,5x lần phù 
hợp với mức dự phóng tăng trưởng 11,6% 
CAGR giai đoạn 2026 – 2028.

Khuyến ngh ị: MUA – Giá m ục tiêu 
25,950 VNĐ/CP  
 Chúng tôi duy trì dự phóng tăng trưởng tín d ụng 2026 đ ạt 27%, với động lực chính 
đến từ phân khúc KHDN, đ ặc biệt là t ừ nhóm FDI trong b ối cảnh dòng v ốn FDI vào 
Việt Nam và hoạt động xuất nhập khẩu duy trì tích cực. Xét về lĩnh vực, cho vay kinh 
doanh BĐS kỳ vọng sẽ tăng trưởng vượt trội khi ngân hàng tham gia vào thu xếp vốn 
cho các dự án hạ tầng và d ự án BĐS ưu tiên , trong khi các m ảng kinh doanh khác 
tăng trưởng ở mức khả quan.

Chúng tôi duy trì dự phóng NIM giảm so với năm 2025 do áp l ực huy động khiến chi 
phí vốn duy trì ở mặt bằng cao, trong khi lãi su ất cho vay dự kiến ở mức cạnh tranh 
để ưu tiên mục tiêu tăng trưởng tín dụng.

Chúng tôi dự phóng tỷ lệ nợ xấu của ngân hàng sẽ tăng 11bps YoY trong bối cảnh lãi 
suất đang neo ở mức cao và kỳ vọng ngân hàng sẽ tiếp tục duy trì chính sách trích 
lập dự phòng thận trọng nhằm củng cố bộ đệm, qua đó hoàn thành mục tiêu duy 
trì tỷ lệ bao phủ nợ xấu ở mức trên 100%.

Dựa trên triển vọng kinh doanh của ngân hàng cũng như đánh giá các y ếu tố rủi ro, 
chúng tôi tiếp tục duy trì khuyến nghị MUA với cổ phiếu MBB với giá mục tiêu 25,950 
VNĐ/CP - tiềm năng tăng giá 26.2% so với giá đóng cửa ngày 17/09/2026.

Ngắn hạn 
(Dưới 3 tháng)

Trung hạn (Từ 3 đến 9 tháng)

Chỉ báo  Nhận định xu hướng Hành động 
Xanh đậm  Khả năng tăng điểm cao Mua mạnh 
Xanh nhạt  Khả năng tăng điểm vừa phải Mua từng phần 
Vàng  Xu hướng trung tính Mua/Bán trading

Ngưỡng kỹ thuật

VNINDEX cho thấy xu hướng hồi phục trong tuần qua nhưng với diễn biến khá giằng co, 
được thể hiện qua sự hình thành của một mẫu nến "spinning" trên đồ thị tuần. Trên khung 
ngày, bức tranh có phần tích cực hơn khi chỉ số đã cho phản ứng với vùng hỗ trợ quanh 
1790 và hồi phục trở lại, phản ánh sự nhập cuộc khá chủ động của lực cầu. Vùng kháng cự 
gần quanh 1850 bắt đầu khiến áp lực bán có phần gia tăng trong phiên cuối tuần và diễn 
biến rung lắc có thể sẽ còn tiếp diễn. Mặc dù vậy, khi xu hướng tăng ngắn hạn đang được 
bảo lưu, nhịp giảm điểm vừa qua nhiều khả năng chỉ là bước điều chỉnh tích lũy trước khi 
VNINDEX thử thách lại và có cơ hội vượt vùng đỉnh cũ. 
Kháng cự xa: 1900 (+-15) 
Kháng cự gần: 1850 (+-10) 
Hỗ trợ gần: 1770 (+-10) 
Hỗ trợ xa: 1715 (+-15)

Danh mục đầu tư mẫu

Phương pháp tiếp cận: 
- Danh mục tập hợp các cổ phiếu tiềm năng nhất được KBSV 
lựa chọn và tái cơ cấu theo tháng. 
- Ngưỡng cắt lỗ cần được cân nhắc cho mỗi mã là -15%. 
- Danh mục giả định luôn duy trì tỷ trọng 100% là cổ phiếu với 
trọng số được phân bổ đều giữa các mã.

Lưu ý:  
- NĐT được khuyến nghị mua tại mức giá bằng hoặc thấp hơn 
giá khuyến nghị mua. 
- Lợi nhuận kỳ vọng được tính theo giá khuyến nghị mua. 
 So sánh hiệu suất với VN30 Index

Danh mục đầu tư mẫu 
Mã Giá đóng cửa tuần Biến động từ đầu tháng Giá khuyến nghị mua Giá mục tiêu Lợi nhuận kỳ vọng Điểm nhấn đầu tư Báo cáo phân tích 
FRT 130.90 -10.3% 124.30 143.00 15.0% 
- Sau khi phủ sóng các thành phố lớn, chuỗi Long Châu đang mở rộng sang các vùng cấp 2, 3. 
Năm 2026 dự kiến mở thêm 400-450 cửa hàng (+20%YoY) với DT trên mỗi cửa hàng cũ tăng 
trưởng 6%-8%/năm 
- Dư địa lấy thêm thị phần từ các cửa hàng nhỏ lẻ vẫn còn tương đối rộng, FRT đặt mục tiêu 
chiếm 50% thị phần doanh thu ngành bán lẻ dược phẩm, từ mức 17%-20% hiện tại 
- Tiềm năng dài hạn từ mảng tiêm chủng tương đối tích cực 
FRT 
MWG 72.50 -3.3% 79.50 121.60 53.0% 
- Chuỗi TGDD+ĐMX tiếp tục duy trì tăng trưởng trung bình 2 chữ số trong 2026 nhờ chu kỳ nâng 
cấp đồ điện tử, chu kỳ Elnino và nhu cầu BĐS bàn giao

## Document 359: Sales Trader Commentary_ TAIEX 49k Wobble - July De Ja Vu, or Just Pausing Before the Santa Rally_ _ GSTW

Source PDF: all report pdf/Sales Trader Commentary_ TAIEX 49k Wobble - July De Ja Vu, or Just Pausing Before the Santa Rally_ _ GSTW.pdf
Report source: line報告備份
Date: 2026-10-13
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Sales Trader Commentary: TAIEX 49k Wobble -
July De Ja Vu, or Just Pausing Before the Santa
Rally? | GSTW
13 October 2026 | 4:11 PM Taiwan Standard Time
Robert Wang
Goldman Sachs (Asia) L.L.C. Taipei Branch
FICC & Equities
Index lost the 49k points threshold and saw the biggest decline on a point basis since
the end of July. Market seemed to lose the steam to break through 50k points after
the past choppy sessions. Is this July de ja vu or some healthy pullback? Foreigners

and domestic investors seem to share different narrative. Check out below for our
investor feedback.
Key Investor Feedback and Observations
Positioning. we saw meaningful outflows today from all kinds of investors today,
again focusing in ex-TSMC large caps. Today foreigners net sold US$5.2bn today,
the second largest in the history (simultaneously drove TWD weakness over 32.23
today). Domestic investors, especially retail, on the other hand, anchored the

market with the record high margin loan balance (as of 10/12/2026). Local funds
remained buyer as well mainly thanks to ETFs inflow.
Macro. CPI headline brought uncertainty and forced global investors to turn
cautious, which was exacerbated by high yields and crude. For Taiwan, AI trade is
relatively resilient to the Middle Eastern tension and high energy prices. However,
at some point, it is going to have a real economic impact either through Fed or

some other macro data.
Sentiment. Surprisingly, people are still bullish in Taiwan, though they also
acknowledge some choppy trading sessions ahead. Local investor feedback is not
calling for capitulation; in fact, most believe this is just another "two-steps forward
one-step backward" cadence towards another record high. Nonetheless, some
retail start taking chips off the table. This echoes our observation among the
foreign investors. Yes, the AI fundamental future is all sunshine, but as we head into

the end of year, it is natural for them to protect their great fun thus far this year
instead of adding on immediate risk. I'd imagine the next few weeks are going to be
full of 1) rapid sector rotation; 2) weak or unsustainable momentum before Santa
comes into town after the midterm election for our year-end rally Christmas gift.
TSMC Earnings and Others. We know the numbers are going to be good, and we
also know people are going to sell-on-facts after TSMC earnings call; recall most

recent one happened right after the meeting that scarred a lot of investors since.
Perhaps this time people started trimming ahead (and maybe too early?), which
provides some window for a quick rebound into small and mid cap. OCP summit is
happening right now, and TPCA Show will start next week. Watch out for single
stock movement mentioned in the conferences.
10/13/26, 4:13 PM Sales Trader Commentary: TAIEX 49k Wobble - July De Ja Vu, or Just Pausing Before the Santa Rally? | GSTW

https://marquee.gs.com/s/content/markets/812c043a-8535-4003-887e-b01f79096974 1/8

### Page 2

Sector. 1) ABF/CCL is still the crowded long sectors for both foreign and domestic
investor, but echoing on above points, investors need more catalysts to add on
risks or drive the re-ratings. 2) Power is another big issues that data centers need
to resolve, power solutions and fuel cells name are also key focus in Taiwan. 3) Let's
not forget about OSATs. We need more equipment and testing capacity to suffice
enlarging demand from both ASIC and GPU.

Key Stocks I Follow: Unimicron, Kinsus, NYPCB, ZDT, EMC, TUC, Delta, Lite-On,
Kaori, ASE, KYEC, All Ring
Risk. Upside looks thin in the short term. Good earnings are not enough - look at
Nanya Tech and Memory sector today. Chasing momentum now may get hurt. I also
smelled some short unwind today (i.e. funding short names outperformance such
as Realtek, Novatek, Shipper, etc) - signs of de-leveraging. Directionless is how I
would describe the past few days, but maybe it's time to find some value Buy?

Max loss amount invested is 100%

## Document 370: The end of the safety net_ Life without the macroeconomic stabilisers (2026-09-28)

Source PDF: all report pdf/The end of the safety net_ Life without the macroeconomic stabilisers (2026-09-28).pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 10
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Deutsche Bank 
Research Institute

The end of the safety net:  
Life without the macroeconomic stabilisers

1 
Authors 
Henry Allen 
Macro Strategist

28 September 2026 
For several decades, financial markets and the wider economy benefited from an implicit 
safety net. If a slowdown or a downturn arrived, there was a monetary put from central 
banks, and a fiscal put from governments. The monetary put would see interest rates 
slashed and liquidity injected via QE. The fiscal put would see governments run larger 
deficits to cushion any downturn. These policy stabilisers smoothed out the business 
cycle, and supported asset valuations too. 
These policy interventions were increasingly normalised from the late-1980s, and became 
more aggressive with each passing cycle. This reached its peak during the Covid-19 
pandemic, when governments ran fiscal deficits unprecedented in peacetime, whilst 
many central banks expanded their balance sheets with huge asset purchases. 
But after an era of ever-bigger interventions, these old stabilisers now face major 
obstacles. On the fiscal side, policy is constrained today by sovereign yields at multi-year 
highs, alongside the highest debt-to-GDP ratios in several decades. Then on the monetary 
side, policy is constrained by persistent inflation that’s increasingly supply-driven. 
This represents a big shift from the last few decades, but it does echo previous regimes 
where the macroeconomic stabilisers were either blunted or left unused. A good example 
is the 1970s experience, when stagflation and supply shocks posed a dilemma to 
traditional policy tools. Even further back, the pre-WWI environment of the classical gold 
standard and balanced budget orthodoxy saw governments play a very different and more 
limited role in macroeconomic stabilisation. 
Looking forward, this environment means policymakers will have a much more difficult 
time stabilising the business cycle in the coming years. So absent a sharp fall in yields or a 
collapse in inflation, higher macro volatility will be here to stay.

### Page 2

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Page 2 Deutsche Bank AG
1. Background: How the stabilisers used to work.
Economic policymakers have long used interventions to stabilise output. This
became increasingly fashionable after WWII, when Keynesian economics
established a more active use for fiscal policy to stimulate the economy in a
downturn. This meant budget deficits became a more regular feature of life, rather
than used solely for wartime emergencies.

It was from the late 1980s, however, that the extent of policy interventions became
increasingly sizeable. Indeed, it was here that the origins of a central bank “put” for
financial markets began, in the crisis of Black Monday in 1987. That was a huge
stock market crash, where the S&P 500 fell over 20% in a single day, marking the
biggest daily decline in its entire history. The size of the falls even went beyond the
Great Depression, raising fears of wider contagion, particularly if it were to spark a

liquidity crisis that caused financial institutions to fail. However, the Federal
Reserve responded by cutting rates and providing liquidity, offering a
macroeconomic stabiliser against the prospect of wider economic stress. This
offered support against a wider downturn, and the US economic expansion
continued until 1990.
Whilst the genesis of a central bank “put” came in 1987, the idea gained further
traction in the late-1990s, with the Asian financial crisis in 1997, followed by the

Russian financial crisis in 1998. Fears of an economic contraction mounted,
particularly after issues at Long-Term Capital Management (LTCM) raised concerns
that the US wasn’t immune from these global pressures. So once again, the Fed
delivered three 25bp rate cuts pre-emptively, seeking to support the economy.
A few years later, the bursting of the dot com bubble in early 2000 set the stage for
yet further stimulus. As equity markets declined, the Fed began to cut rates from

early 2001, ultimately delivering 475bps of cuts that year that took rates from 6.5%
to just 1.75%. That was followed up by further rate cuts in 2002 and 2003, leaving
rates at 1% by mid-2003 into 2004. Moreover, the Bush tax cuts were also delivered
in 2001 and 2003, acting as a fiscal stimulus as the wider US economy entered
recession.
In hindsight, these moves seem modest compared to what then happened in 2008,
when the Global Financial Crisis led to even bigger policy interventions. Suddenly,

the world faced the biggest economic contraction since the Great Depression, with
genuine fears that a repeat might happen. To avoid this, policymakers launched into
the biggest economic stimulus ever seen in a peacetime environment. That
included massive fiscal stimulus, with the US federal deficit at 9.8% of GDP in 2008,
the highest since 1945. Moreover, central banks slashed rates to the zero lower
bound, and many like the Federal Reserve launched into Quantitative Easing

programmes, purchasing assets to ease financial conditions even more. Over the
coming years, many began pledging to keep rates low well into the future,
pioneering the concept of forward guidance.
These levels of policy intervention weren’t confined to the immediate crisis
however. In the subsequent years, active attempts were made to prolong and
extend the business cycle, particularly as the 2010s became an era of low inflation
and low interest rates. In this environment, borrowing was historically cheap, and

low inflation meant central banks could cut rates without seeing a big overshoot of
their targets. Indeed, both the Fed and the ECB undershot their 2% inflation target
on average in the 2010s, and the ECB took their own deposit rate into negative
territory in 2014.

### Page 3

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 3

targets on the downside in the 2010s (PCE inflation for the US, HICP inflation for

Source : Haver Analytics, Deutsche Bank
By 2019, the year before the pandemic, the intellectual climate had continued to
move in the direction of more policy intervention. Government bond yields hit new

lows, with Germany’s 10-year bund yield at -0.7% in summer 2019. This
environment of negative yields meant borrowing was basically a “free lunch”, and
that summer also saw the Fed begin a 75bp easing cycle, as inflation was below
their target and they wanted to sustain the expansion.
The peak of this policy interventionism was seen with the Covid-19 pandemic in
2020. Just as in 2008, the world was facing a huge and sudden economic
contraction. Moreover, the consensus among policymakers was that they could

have done more to speed up the recovery after 2008, potentially avoiding years of
very high unemployment. As such, 2020 saw the most aggressive economic
stimulus packages ever seen in a peacetime environment, well above those in 2008.
Central banks immediately slashed rates back to the zero lower bound, pledged to
keep rates low for years to come, and launched into major QE programmes again.
Then on the fiscal side, record peacetime deficits were run. Indeed, the US federal

deficit of 14.5% of GDP in 2020 was the highest since 1945.

### Page 4

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Page 4 Deutsche Bank AG
During the pandemic period, many initially expected these interventions to remain
for years to come. After all, in mid-2020, financial markets continued to price a
continuation of the low inflation and low interest rates of the 2010s. This belief was
clear on several fronts, and the 10-year Treasury yield closed at just 0.51% in August
2020. Meanwhile, the Federal Reserve even adjusted its own framework to allow for

average inflation targeting, i.e. permitting an overshoot of the inflation target after
periods of an undershoot. This reflected the consensus view that inflation was
unlikely to be a serious threat.
But as we now know, inflation returned in a serious way after the pandemic, forcing
central banks into rate hikes. Meanwhile, public debt-to-GDP ratios rose
significantly thanks to the pandemic stimulus, helping to push up sovereign bond
yields too. In other words, many of the usual policy tools of the previous few

decades were either blunted or no longer available.
2. The Monetary Constraint: High inflation means the central
bank put isn’t available
In the pre-2020 world described above, central banks regularly grappled with how
to generate enough inflation. In part, this was thanks to several structural forces
that put downward pressure on global prices. For instance, deepening
globalisation acted as a positive supply shock that kept costs down. Meanwhile, the

expansion of the global labour force in these years also boosted the supply side. In
other words, inflation was low and central banks generally had the space to cut
interest rates when they needed to.
But since the pandemic, we have entered a very different environment of above-
target inflation across many of the world’s biggest economies. Initially, that was
caused by a huge wave of pent-up demand after the pandemic, alongside
disruption to supply chains and labour markets. But the subsequent years also

brought further shocks. Those included Russia’s invasion of Ukraine in early 2022,
driving a big spike in European energy prices, and pushing Euro Area inflation above
10% briefly. In 2026, the closure of the Strait of Hormuz then acted as a fresh energy
shock, pushing inflation further away from target and contributing to a fresh
tightening cycle from the major central banks.
Figure 4: In the 2020s, inflation has generally been above central bank targets

### Page 5

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 5
Alongside those shocks, the structural forces keeping inflation down have gone
into reverse. For instance, demographic trends have seen working-age populations
shrink in many places. Meanwhile, the trend towards deeper globalisation has
stalled, and the focus has turned to near-shoring and supply-chain resilience, rather
than locating production in the cheapest place. Simultaneously, other trends like

the push for higher defence spending are also adding to demand-side pressures.
Because of all this, the 2020s so far have brought persistently above-target inflation,
and central bankers have found it much more difficult to ease policy in response to
growth wobbles or financial stability fears.
This initially became apparent in 2022, amidst the biggest inflation wave for several
decades. Central banks had to keep hiking rates, even in the face of slowing growth

and financial market stress. Indeed, despite the S&P 500 being in bear market
territory by mid-2022, the Federal Reserve still launched into a series of 75bp hikes,
the biggest hikes since the 1990s. Then in 2023, despite the collapse of Silicon
Valley Bank and fears of wider financial contagion, the Fed and ECB still continued
with their rate hikes to combat inflation.
That tightening cycle in 2022-23 was a significant moment, because high inflation

meant monetary policy could not simply act as a stabiliser against growth or
financial stress. After all, policymakers realised that if they didn’t act to tackle high
inflation, they risked a repeat of the 1970s and the potential for even harsher
monetary tightening to get inflation under control again.
Today in 2026, this is a familiar problem again. We are in a globally synchronised
hiking cycle, with the Fed, ECB and Bank of Japan all having delivered rate hikes.

So far at least, global growth has been robust and financial conditions remain
accommodative by historical standards. But because inflation remains clearly
above target, central banks don’t have the space they did in the pre-pandemic world
to simply cut rates at the first sign of difficulty. There is still an inflation mandate to
be achieved, and given we have now seen multiple years of elevated inflation, there
is a much higher risk of inflation expectations becoming unanchored in that

context, particularly if another shock occurred.
3. The Fiscal Constraint: High yields and high public debt
mean fiscal stimulus isn’t easily available either
Just as monetary policy is constrained by high inflation, fiscal policy today is also
constrained by higher yields and higher public debt. This is a big transition from the
world of 1980-2020, when yields were generally falling throughout. Indeed, despite
all the fiscal stimulus around the GFC, the continuous decline in yields over the

2010s meant that debt dynamics remained manageable, because lower yields
helped to offset the impact of higher debt.
Since the pandemic however, we’ve been in a very different world, as the dual
forces of higher debt and higher yields have made debt dynamics far more
unfavourable around the world. And this isn’t just a nominal story, as real yields
have also risen to multi-year highs as well.

### Page 6

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Page 6 Deutsche Bank AG
Figure 5: 10yr Government Bond Yields (%) have risen to multi-year highs across
several countries
-1
0

5

United States Germany France Japan UK
Source : Bloomberg Finance LP, Deutsche Bank
In essence, there are 4 new constraints around fiscal stimulus.
First, is the significant rise in global yields. By definition, the cost of incurring debt
has grown, in both nominal and real terms. Relative to the pre-pandemic baseline

in early 2020, the 10-year US Treasury yield has risen from 1.9% to 5.2%. Similarly
in Germany, the 10-year bund yield has risen from -0.2% to 3.6%. Moreover, with
yields already elevated, any further borrowing risks crowding out the private sector,
and further dampening growth. And for governments, interest spending has
ramped up significantly. For instance, US net interest spending as a share of GDP
exceeded its recent peak from the early 1990s in the most recent fiscal year, and the

CBO forecast this is set to continue.
Figure 6: US Net Interest Costs (% of GDP) with CBO Forecasts
0
1
2
3
4
5
6
7
1970 1980 1990 2000 2010 2020 2030 2040 2050
Source : CBO, Deutsche Bank
The second constraint is that public debt itself is now at a higher level. This interacts
with the higher yields mentioned above, because even though yields have only
returned to their levels from the mid-2000s, that is much more impactful because
they are being paid on a much larger debt pile. Using IMF numbers for consistency,

net public debt across the advanced economies has risen from 52% of GDP in 2000,
to 80% in 2025, with further increases projected to 2030. This means the fiscal
space available to governments is more limited than it used to be.

### Page 7

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 7
The third constraint on fiscal stimulus is the high starting level of budget deficits. So
to generate a meaningful fiscal stimulus, it would require deficits to be pushed up
to even higher levels than they are today. But that in turn would add to the risks of
bond market disruption that cascades through the economy, leading to higher
borrowing costs for corporates and consumers as well.
The fourth constraint is higher inflation. When inflation is high, any fiscal stimulus

that boosts demand risks pushing up prices rather than boosting output, and in turn
makes monetary tightening more likely that could offset the impact of the stimulus.
This trade-off was apparent in 2022, when central banks were hiking rates
aggressively, and even further back in the inflationary environment of the 1970s. In
a world of high inflation and supply constraints, fiscal stimulus is more likely to
overheat the economy.
4. Are there any other eras of history where the stabilisers

weren’t available?
Whilst history never quite repeats exactly, there have been other eras in which the
traditional policy stabilisers were either unavailable or severely blunted, which hold
some lessons for our own time.
The pre-WWI classical gold standard
Before WWI, the international monetary system was known as the gold standard,
in which currencies were convertible into a fixed quantity of gold. In practice
however, this acted as a huge constraint on macroeconomic stabilisation. After all,

monetary policy had to maintain the convertibility of gold at a fixed parity. This
meant contemporary options like QE weren’t available in a crisis. Meanwhile on the
fiscal side, governments generally adhered to balanced budget orthodoxy. This was
before Keynes wrote the General Theory, so the modern understanding of
countercyclical deficit spending was not in place. In the US, there wasn’t even a
central bank until the Federal Reserve’s establishment in 1913.

Figure 7: Gold Prices (US Dollars per ounce, log scale)
1
10
100
1000
10000

Under the classical gold standard,

parity
Source : Finaeon, Deutsche Bank

### Page 8

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Page 8 Deutsche Bank AG
Without modern policy stabilisers however, this was also an era of huge
macroeconomic volatility. There was a major financial panic across Europe and the
US in 1873, which marked the start of the so-called “Long Depression”. In the US,
that contraction lasted until 1879, which in length was even longer than the Great
Depression contraction of 1929-33. And this was followed by other major panics,

including the 1893 panic that led to another US contraction, along with the 1907
panic that saw the Dow Jones almost halve relative to its 1906 peak.
The stagflationary 1970s
The more recent example comes from the 1970s, another era of multiple supply
shocks. The biggest were the two oil shocks in 1973 and 1979, which both
represented negative supply shocks that pushed up inflation and hurt growth in the
US and Europe. However, this meant policymakers faced a dilemma. If central

banks cut rates, that would add to the inflationary pressures and risk worsening the
wage-price spirals already underway. Meanwhile, if fiscal stimulus were pursued,
that would also push up inflation.
Figure 8: US CPI (year-on-year % change)
-2
0
2
4
6
8
10
12
14
16
1955 1960 1965 1970 1975 1980 1985
First Oil Shock
Second Oil Shock
Source : Haver Analytics, Deutsche Bank
This context meant that policy couldn’t smooth out the business cycle as
successfully as it had in other decades. Indeed, in the decade from the first oil shock

in 1973, the US experienced 3 separate recessions. That is significantly more
turbulent than the last 40 years, when there have been just 4 recessions in total, or
once a decade on average. For financial markets too, the 1970s were a terrible
period for asset performance, with both equities and bonds losing ground in real
terms across multiple countries.
5. Implications
If economic policymakers can’t use traditional monetary and fiscal policy tools to

stabilise output and smooth the business cycle, then this will result in higher macro
volatility.
If we look back at the last 40 years, it is notable that the 5 most recent US expansions
all rank in the top 7 longest expansions since business-cycle dating began. This has
several causes, and some of it reflects a move away from an agriculture-based
economy where output was dependent on factors like the weather. But a key role
has also been played by pre-emptive policy interventions, such as cutting rates to

stop a slowdown turning into a recession. Indeed, recent examples include the a
dovish shifts in monetary policy after the 1987 stock market crash, the 1997-98

### Page 9

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 9
financial crises, and the mid-cycle slowdown of 2015-16.
Beyond the actual policy interventions, these moves also had a confidence effect
that supported financial markets. The fact that policymakers stood ready to take
action helped create a virtuous circle, as optimism on the economic outlook
boosted financial asset values, supported wealth effects, and avoided a tightening
in financial conditions that would itself cause growth to slow.

Clearly, policy is not as constrained today as in some periods of history. There is no

longer a gold standard where the convertibility needs to be maintained. And unlike
the 1970s, inflation is not in double-digits. Nevertheless, this is still a more
constrained world than we have been used to in the last 4 decades. Higher inflation
and more frequent supply shocks are constraining monetary policy. Meanwhile,
higher yields and higher public debt levels are constraining fiscal policy. We have
already seen the impacts since the pandemic, most notably in 2022, when central

banks hiked rates to combat the inflation surge, and there was a simultaneous
selloff across bonds and equities of a kind unseen in modern times.
Looking forward, this environment will offer new challenges of the sort we haven't
been used to in recent decades. The policy trade-offs are now more acute, and if
pre-emptive stabilisation is no longer available, then higher macro volatility lies
ahead.

### Page 3

Helen Belopolsky, Miha Hribernik | This Month in Geopolitics | October 2026
Deutsche Bank
Research Institute
Diplomacy appears firmly back in play. The past month saw continued fighting and renewed diplomacy.
Reporting suggests that the US is prepared to extend troop deployments in the Middle East to 2027. Iran
targeted both US forces in the region and some tankers moving through the Strait, but has been unable to
prevent a significant rebound in crude flows through Hormuz. On 22 SEP, talks were mediated by Qatar,

Pakistan and Egypt, and on 24 SEP, Iran presented the US with a seven-day roadmap to a ceasefire leading
to a phased reopening of the Strait of Hormuz and a return to nuclear negotiations, conditional on a US
lifting of its blockade of Iran, sanctions relief and broader cessation of hostilities. The Trump
Administration rejected the proposal, but negotiations continue.
To watch will be whether October is the month the war transitions into a negotiated settlement, which

may hinge on whether Iran is willing to make some concessions on sanctions relief and the nuclear
question, and whether Trump is keen to demonstrate that the war has produced a political and economic
result in the final weeks before the 3 NOV midterm elections. It is plausible that the current diplomatic
channel could produce a temporary de-escalation mechanism as long as there is pathway around the
broader nuclear issue.
Both sides will reconvene for trade talks in Beijing on 8-9 October, which the EU views as a deadline for

“tangible results”. Brussels remains focused on its rising trade deficit with China – which has reached a
record €1 billion per day – and the impact of Chinese goods on European industry. The bloc has vowed to
take measures if talks fail.
While both sides could achieve progress in talks, a lasting breakthrough appears improbable, setting the
stage for more trade tensions into 2027. New EU trade investigations, curbs on Chinese imports, and EU-
level instruments intended to address alleged Chinese overcapacity, are possible outcomes, to which

Beijing has stated it would respond.
China-EU trade talks could also impact China-US relations. Should talks with Brussels fall short of averting
increased European trade pressure, we assess that Beijing may further prioritise near-term US stability. See
Rules of Engagement: Mapping the Next Phase of China-US Ties for more.

Latvia will elect all 100 members of the 15th Saeima. Latvia, which borders Russia and Belarus, has been
among the most vocal supporters of Ukraine. The next government will set the country’s course on
defence spending, Ukraine aid, sanctions enforcement and border security. The field is highly fragmented
with the governing parties lacking a majority in the polls. The fragmentation matters as a weakened and

multi-party scramble could mean a slower, more unstable coalition.
Brazil’s first electoral round is on 4 OCT, with a runoff on 25 OCT. This is a critical election as it will
decide whether Latin America’s biggest economy continues President Lula’s state-led social spending or
pivots to a more fiscally-orthodox agenda under Bolsonaro. Polls have gone from a Lula lead in the spring
to a statistical dead heat between Lula and Bolsonaro. A Brazilian Supreme Court crisis – which has

implicated Supreme Court Justices and politicians on both sides – is weighing on the race. Congressional
and gubernatorial races will also impact the governability of the country.
This election will also shape whether the country hedges between Washington and Beijing under Lula or
tilts toward closer US trade and security alignment under Bolsonaro with potential implications for
tariffs, BRICS+ cohesion, and Latin America’s broader positioning between the two powers.

The meetings will take place in Bangkok, bringing finance ministers, central bankers, policy-makers and
business leaders together. The agenda is expected to centre on global growth, financial stability, debt,
trade and investment, development finance and the impact of AI on jobs and productivity, with particular
attention given to mobilizing private capital. The key watchpoint will be the outlook for global growth
and inflation, debt vulnerabilities, the future of the international financial architecture and how policy-

makers respond to increasing fragmentation in trade and capital flows.

Leaders are expected to focus heavily on Ukraine, European security and defence, competitiveness and
the EU’s economic position in a more contested global environment. They will also take forward
negotiations on the 2028-2034 EU long-term budget. They set themselves a soft deadline in June to get
the budget broadly locked down because missing it pushes ratification into 2027 and risks a funding gap

for beneficiaries from JAN 2028. To watch will be a genuine breakthrough on the multiannual framework
or more likely what “substantial” progress may look like. Ukraine funding is the other pressure point. This
meeting will be important for understanding the direction of European defence spending and industrial
policy, the next phase of budget negotiations and the EU response to growing external economic
pressure and strategic dependencies.
The Canadian province of Alberta will hold a referendum, which includes a question about the province’s

future within Canada. This is not an independence vote itself but asking voters to choose between
remaining a Canadian province and asking the Alberta government to begin the constitutional process
required for a future binding referendum on separation. Current polling suggests “remain” is comfortably
ahead. The significance is around potential renewed uncertainty around Canada’s federal cohesion,
energy policy, fiscal transfers and Alberta's relationship with Ottawa and Washington. Even a modest

agreement to begin a process towards secession would likely keep alive equalization grievances as a
structural fault line in Canadian federalism feeding into political risk.

The event’s theme focuses on “Advancing Alliances in a New Era.” It will tackle how governments and
businesses respond to a world where geopolitics, economic security, supply chains and technology are
increasingly intertwined. There is likely to be a strong focus on the future of the transatlantic

relationship, including how Europe adapts to a more demanding security environment. There will also be
discussions of tariffs, supply chain resilience, critical capabilities and the weaponization of economic
interdependence. It will align with Chancellor Merz’s efforts to ensure Europe actively shapes outcomes
around strategic autonomy and reducing China exposure in manufacturing.

The second summit will take place in Saudi Arabi, bringing EU and Gulf leaders together. The agenda is

particularly salient given the current crisis in the region. Economically, the relationship is substantial: EU-

While fifth plenums historically focus on economic planning, the development guidelines for the 15th
Five-Year Plan (2026–2030) were already adopted during the Fourth Plenum in OCT 2025, allowing the
upcoming session to focus on personnel and governance matters.

Elections will be held for the 26th Knesset, the first since the October 7th attacks and wars in Gaza,

Naftali Bennett’s new “Together” party is polling as the main rival bloc, alongside Gadi Eisenkot’s Yashar,

and West Bank settlement activity.

become the EU’s first “associate member”.

調査レポート

今月のグラフ（2026 年 10 月）

制度変更が押し上げた耐久財需要

調査部 研究員 小林 啓介

2026 年 4～6 月期の実質 GDP における個人消費は前期比＋0.0%と横ばいにとどまったものの、耐久財は同
＋4.0%と堅調に増加した。特に自動車とエアコンの需要が大きく高まったことが、押し上げに寄与したとみられる。 
新車販売台数（除く軽）は、自動車の燃費性能に応じて取得時に課税される「環境性能割」が 2025 年度末に
廃止されることを受けて今年度に需要の一部が先送りされた結果、4 月に前月比＋25.7%と急増した。同様にエ
アコンの出荷台数も、2027 年度から省エネ基準が厳しくなり、製品価格の上昇が予想されることから、それ を見
越した駆け込み需要が生じ、4 月には前月比＋12.5%と急増した。 
もっとも、こうした制度・規制変更に起因する一時的な需要増は徐々に落ち着きつつある。自動車とエアコンは
いずれも依然として高水準ではあるものの、4 月をピークに減少傾向に転じている。雇用・所得環境は良好であり、
イラン情勢悪化の影響で落ち込んでいた 消費者マインドも持ち直しへ向かっているなど、 個人消費を取り巻く環
境は悪くない。このため、7～9 月期の個人消費は底堅く推移するとみられるものの、耐久財消費の一時的な押し
上げ効果が剥落することで、小幅な伸びにとどまる可能性があることには注意を要しよう。

図表 新車販売台数（除く軽）とエアコン出荷台数

## Document 580: 利上げで悪化する一部AI関連企業の資金調達環境 ～半導体市場に影響も、中長期的な市場拡大は変わらず～

Source PDF: all report pdf/利上げで悪化する一部AI関連企業の資金調達環境 ～半導体市場に影響も、中長期的な市場拡大は変わらず～.pdf
Report source: DLRI
Date: 2026-10-16
Pages: 4
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Investment Navigator

2026 年 10 月 16 日

1/4 
利上げで悪化する一部 AI 関連企業の資金調達環境 
～半導体市場に影響も、中長期的な市場拡大は変わらず～ 
 経済調査部 シニア・フェロー  嶌峰 義清 
（℡：050-5474-7489）

（要旨） 
○ FRB が9 月に3 年 2 カ月ぶりの利上げに踏み切り、 政策金利が中立金利の水準に近づくなか、
AI 関連企業の資金調達環境に変化が出てきた。 データセンターを借入で建設するネオクラウ
ド企業の社債利回りは15%近くまで上昇し、市場では信用不安の様相も見られる。 
○ 韓国の出荷・在庫バランスは、半導体、電子部品ともに悪化した。台湾でも電子・光学産業
の受注の勢いが鈍り、顧客在庫が「過剰」に転じるなど、需給が変調する兆しが出ている。 
○ それでも、 ハイパースケーラーの設備投資やメモリーの供給不足をみる限り、AI 関連需要の
拡大は中長期的に続く可能性が高い。今後は「AI ならば何でも買われる」局面から、財務の
健全性と需給をもとに銘柄が選別される局面に移ると考えられる。

1.  利上げが中立水準に届き、市場の前提が変わる 
FRBは9 月のFOMCで、政策金利の誘導目標を0.25％引き上げ、3.75～4.00％とした（図表1）。
利上げの背景にあるのは、根強いイ
ンフレだ。9 月の消費者物価指数は前
年比＋3.7％と市場予想を上回った。
市場では10 月27～28 日のFOMCで追
加利上げがあるとの見方が再び強ま
りつつある。金利の上昇は国債市場
でも顕著で、2 年債利回りは5％に迫
り、10 年債利回りも5.3％台まで上
昇している。 
FRBは、長期的に望ましい政策金利
（景気を刺激も抑制もしない中立金
利）を 3.25～3.50％とみている。足
元の政策金利はすでにその水準を超
えており、金融政策は景気を抑制す
注：色つき部分はFRBによる中立金利水準域
出所：FRB
（図表1）米国の政策金利（FF金利）の推移

### Page 2

2/4 
る領域に入っている。金融引き締めが実体経済に効いてくるまでには通常1～2 年かかる。それで
も、景気の先行きを読む金融市場は、早い段階で影響を受けやすい。 
なかでも金利上昇の影響を受けやすいのが、高い成長期待を背景に株価が形成されてきたAI・
半導体関連株だ。将来の利益への期待が大きい企業ほど、割引率が上がると理論的な株価の下落
幅が大きくなる。加えて今回は、金利上昇が株価評価の調整にとどまらず、AI投資そのものを支
える資金調達の経路にまで影響し始めた点に注意が必要だ。

2.  データセンター投資企業の社債利回りが急騰 
その象徴となったのが、AI向けクラウド事業者であるCoreWeaveの社債だ。10 月14 日、同社が
発行した2032 年満期債は14.8％程度まで上昇し、株価は1 日で15％超下落した。同日には
Oracleの株価も9％近く下落した。これを受けて、10 月 15 日の日経平均株価は前日比▲3318.34
円（▲4.9％）と、過去3 番目となる下落幅を記録した。指数への寄与が大きい半導体関連株の下
げが主導したもので、日経半導体株指数は同▲10.1％と7 月 28 日以来の下落率を記録している。 
10 月14 日のCoreWeave社の 2032 年満期債（利率6.25％）の利回りは7.24％まで上昇した。ハ
イパースケーラー（大手クラウド事業者）の大量の社債発行が続くなか、9 月下旬時点で、AI関連
の発行体の社債スプレッドは約115bp（Goldman Sachs集計）に広がった。一方、投資適格社債全
体は 83bp（ICE BofA）と、年初からほぼ横ばいにとどまっている。つまり、社債市場全体の信用
環境は比較的安定しているが、AI関連の発行体だけが選別され、より高い上乗せ金利を求められ
ている。 
背景にあるのは、社債の供給量の多さだ。Alphabet、Amazon、Meta、Oracleの 4 社による社債
発行額は、10 月13 日時点で2,280 億ドルを超えた。これは2025 年の年間発行額の2 倍以上にあ
たる。2027 年にはハイパースケーラー全体の発行額が4,200 億ドルと、2026 年を6 割上回るとの
予想もある。投資対象が同業種の社債に偏ることを回避するため「AI以外の社債を求める」動き
も目立ち始めた。実際、9 月のAI関連の社債発行額は8 月の半分以下に落ち込んだ。 
こうした変化は、AI投資の資金調達に「量」と「価格」の両面から制約が生じ始めたことを示
している。巨額の手元資金を持つ大手ハイパースケーラーにとって、利回りの上昇は直ちに投資
を抑える要因にはならないだろう。一方、借入に頼ってデータセンターを建設するネオクラウド
企業は事情が異なる。資金調達コストの上昇は、事業計画の見直しにつながりやすい。AI投資の
「周辺部」から設備投資のペースが鈍れば、それは半導体需要の伸びの鈍化として波及していく
ことになる。

3.  韓国・台湾で出荷・在庫バランスに変調 
実際、半導体の需給にも変化の兆しが出ている。韓国の8 月の鉱工業生産統計で前年比をみる
と、半導体は出荷の伸びが＋2.5％にとどまる一方、在庫は＋8.8％増加した。出荷の伸びから在
庫の伸びを差し引いた出荷・在庫バランスは▲6.3 ポイントと、在庫の積み上がりが出荷を上回っ
ている。電子部品はさらに悪化が鮮明だ。出荷が前年比▲5.1％減少する一方、在庫は＋16.2％増

### Page 3

3/4 
加し、出荷・在庫バランスは▲21.3 ポイントまで落ち込んだ。台湾の電子部品の出荷・在庫バラ
ンスも▲13.0 ポイント（7 月）とマイナス圏に沈んでいる（図表2）。 
韓国の8 月の半導体輸出額は前年比3 倍を超え、過去最高を更新した。それにもかかわらず出
荷の伸びが2％台にとどまっていることは、輸出額の急増の多くが、メモリーをはじめとする価格
の上昇によってもたらされていることを示唆している。数量の面では、需要の勢いはすでに鈍り
始めている可能性がある。 
台湾でも同様の兆しがある。8 月の電子部品の生産指数は過去最高を更新したが、サーバー製品
の仕様変更や生産計画の調整を受け、伸びは政府の予想を下回った。より先行的な指標である9
月の製造業PMIを電子・光学産業についてみると、次のような変化が出ている。 
✓ 新規受注指数が10 ポイント以上低下 
✓ 受注残指数も10 ポイント以上下がり、54.3％と2025 年 12 月以来の低い水準になった 
✓ 在庫指数は64.8％へ上昇 
✓ 顧客在庫指数は51.0％と、2022 年12 月以来の高い水準で「過剰」に転じた 
一部の部品だけが不足し、ほかの部品が余るミスマッチも広がっている。そのため、受注と生
産がともに急拡大していた局面から、高水準ながら受注の勢いが鈍り、在庫の負担が増す局面へ
と移りつつある。 
この変調の背景には、AIの外側の需要が弱っていることもある。メモリー価格の急騰は、スマ
ートフォンやパソコンなど消費者向け電子機器のコストを押し上げている。スマートフォンなど
では値上げも相次いでいる。値上げによって買い替えの周期が長くなれば、AI以外の用途の半導
体や電子部品の需要が弱まり、在庫調整の動きが広がる可能性がある。

4.  それでも中長期のAI需要拡大は続く 
もっとも、こうした兆しをもって、AI関連需要の拡大が終わったと判断するのは早計だろう。 
※出荷在庫バランスは［出荷（前年比）－在庫（前年比）］
出所：Datastream
（図表2）IT財の出荷在庫バランス

### Page 4

本資料は情報提供を目的として作成されたものであり、投資勧誘を目的としたものではありません。作成時点で、第一ライフ資産運用
経済研究所が信ずるに足ると判断した情報に基づき作成していますが、その正確性、完全性に対する責任は負いません。見通しは予告
なく変更されることがあります。また、記載された内容は、第一生命保険ないしはその関連会社の投資方針と常に整合的であるとは限
りません。 
4/4 
第一に、需要の中心であるハイパースケーラーの投資意欲は依然として強い。ハイパースケー
ラーの設備投資額は2026 年に 8,000 億ドル規模に達し、2027 年には1 兆ドルを超えるとの見方が
出ている。大手5 社の設備投資の米国GDPに対する比率は、2026 年の2.4％から 2027 年には3.2％
に上昇すると見込まれる。ITバブル期（90 年代後半から2000 年頃）のインターネット網整備（ピ
ーク時で1.2％）をはるかに上回る規模だ。 
第二に、供給側の制約は依然として強い。サムスン電子とSKハイニックスのメモリー在庫は10
日分を下回ったとの推計もあり、米マイクロンは2028 年度まで需給の逼迫が続くとの見方を示し
ている。台湾のTSMCの9 月の売上高は前年比54.6％増と、9 月として過去最高を記録した。エヌ
ビディアも次の四半期に1,080 億ドルの売上高を見込んでいる。韓国の半導体在庫の増加には、
休暇シーズンや生産計画の調整に伴う一時的な面も含まれている。月次の統計の振れも大きいた
め、1～2 カ月のデータだけで循環的な調整局面入りと結論づけることはできない。 
一方で、投資の規模が大きくなるにつれ、それを支える資金面での余裕は薄くなっている。大
手5 社でさえ、2027 年から2028 年にかけてはAI投資の支出が営業キャッシュフローを上回ると見
込まれている。営業キャッシュフローがプラスに転じるのは2029 年と予想されている。つまりAI
投資は、手元資金だけで賄える段階から、外部からの資金調達に頼る段階に入りつつある。金利
上昇と社債市場での選別が強まれば、投資計画が「中止」されることはなくても、その「時期」
や「ペース」が見直される可能性は否定できない。 
以上を踏まえると、AI・半導体株を取り巻く環境は、成長期待だけで株価が上昇してきた局面
から、次の三つの要素を同時に見極めなければならない局面に移りつつあるといえる。 
① 金融政策が引き締め方向に転じたことによる株価評価の調整 
② 資金調達コストの上昇による投資ペースの鈍化 
③ 出荷・在庫バランスの悪化に表れる需給の変調 
利上げを実施すれば2 会合連続となる10 月末のFOMCは、市場の方向感を左右する可能性があ
る。利上げを実施すれば、FRBは多少の景気減速を厭わない姿勢を見せることに繋がるため、市場
の警戒感は一気に高まろう。社債市場においては、格付け感のスプレッドが更に拡大すると見込
まれ、特にAI関連ではその格差が一層大きくなることが懸念される。 
ただし、AIが中長期の成長分野であるという見方そのものが揺らいでいるわけではない。今後
は、同じAI関連の企業の間でも差がつきやすくなるだろう。厚い手元資金を持つ大手ハイパース
ケーラーや、供給不足が続くメモリー・先端ロジック・先端パッケージは、相対的に底堅い動き
が期待できる。一方で、借入に頼って投資を拡大してきた企業や、AI以外の用途への依存度が高
い電子部品分野は、調整の圧力を受けやすい。 
半導体関連の比重が高い日本株、とりわけ日経平均株価にとっても、こうした選別の動きは無
縁ではない。中長期の拡大基調を前提としつつも、短期的には在庫調整と株価評価の調整が重な
る可能性に留意する必要があるだろう。

以 上

原油
(株)日本総合研究所 原油市場展望 2026年９月
◆現状：100ドルを挟んで乱高下
８月のWTI原油先物価格は 、中旬に80ドル台
後半に上昇。米国とイランの交渉が難航したほか 、
イエメンの武装組織フーシがタンカー等を攻撃したこと
で、石油供給の減少が意識されたことが背景。下旬
には、80ドル台前半に下落 。ウォーシュ議長のタカ
派的な発言を受けて、FRBの利上げ観測が強まり、
米国需要が伸び悩むとの見方が広がったことが主因 。
８月末から９月中旬にかけて 、原油価格は100
ドル台に急騰。米国とイランの間で攻撃の応酬が続
いたことが背景。加えて、フーシがバブ・エル・マンデブ
海峡付近の港湾都市を掌握したことや 、サウジアラ
ビアの東西パイプラインが攻撃を受けて操業を停止し
たことで、ホルムズ海峡を迂回するルートからの石油
輸出が停滞するとの懸念も価格を押し上げ。下旬に
は、東西パイプラインの輸送能力が早期に回復する
との報道を材料に、原油価格は90ドル台に下落。
◆投機筋のネットポジションは小幅に拡大

投機筋の買い越し幅は拡大。中東情勢の緊迫化
を受けて、原油価格の上昇を見込む投資家が増加
している可能性。
◆見通し：当面100ドル前後で高止まり
両国の戦闘がこれ以上激化しないことを前提とす
るメインシナリオでは、原油価格が100ドルを中心と
する高値圏で推移する 見通し。ホルムズ海峡の封
鎖は続く一方、東西パイプラインなどの代替ルートや 、
中東産油国の生産が徐々に回復することが背景。

### Page 4

原油
(株)日本総合研究所 原油市場展望 2026年９月3
◆米国のガソリン・ディーゼル価格が高騰
米国では 、燃料油の価格が高騰 。足元のレギュ
ラーガソリン小売価格は全米平均で 4.5ドル/ガロン
と、消費者の心理的節目とされる４ドルを超える水
準。トラックや農業機械の燃料となるディーゼルの価
格は、統計開始以来の最高値に上昇。
◆製油業者のマージン確保や増税も影響
こうした石油製品価格が高騰する一因は 、原料と
なる原油の価格上昇。ただし、次の２点が、過去の
原油高局面よりも製品価格を高める方向に作用。
第１に、製油業者による強気のマージン設定 。世
界的な石油製品の需給ひっ迫が主因 。ホルムズ海
峡の封鎖を受けて 、中東からの製品輸出が減少し
たほか、主に中東産原油を精製していたアジアからの
輸出も減少。ウクライナによる石油関連インフラへの
攻撃を背景に、ロシアからの輸出も下振れ。
第２に、州政府による増税。連邦政府は、1993
年の増税以降、燃料税を据え置いている一方、多く
の州は、環境対策などの観点から増税を実施 。直

近の原油高局面（2022年）に比べて、州政府に
よる増税分が小売価格を押し上げる構図に。
◆燃料高は、米中間選挙で共和党の逆風に
燃料価格の高騰は、11月に実施される米中間選
挙において、現在の与党である共和党の逆風となる
可能性。ガソリン高が無党派層の不満を強めるほか、
ディーゼル高がトラック運転手や農家などの共和党の
伝統的な支持基盤の反発を招く可能性も。
トピック：米国のガソリン・ディーゼル価格が高騰する背景

然而隨著美德利差短期飆升過快 ，先前漲勢兇猛的美元
或暫時歇息 ，維持高檔盤整 。然而，法國政局不確定性
恐一路拖至 2027年6月，中長期資金仍可能持續從歐洲

流入美國 ， 支撐美元長期走勢 。 本週美元指數區間
100.5~103。
 債市展望：儘管法國、美國、日本政治官員相繼釋出試
圖穩定市場情緒的財政計畫或談話 ，但市場反應明顯不
買單，願景與口號終究無法安撫投資人信心 ，殖利率短
期 仍 有 續 攀 升 風 險， 本 週 美 債 十 年 殖 利 率 區 間 看 在
5.2%~5.4%。
 股市展望  : 3Q26財報季將至 ，市場預估 S&P500整體
EPS年增率高達 24.5%，強 勁 的 盈 餘成 長 依 舊是 支撐
4Q26股市創高關鍵 ，惟短期仍須留意利率攀升對於市
場廣度的衝擊。本週標普500指數區間7,650~7,950。

產業訊息
1. Marvell於投資人日上調2028FY營收預期至200億美元，
同時首度釋出2031FY營收目標700~900億美元。公司認

為AI潛在市場規模至2030年有望達到4,000億美元，主要
由Google、微軟、亞馬遜等超大規模雲端服務商自研客
製化晶片帶動。(詳見Page 5)
2. Google再度擴大核能布局 ，與Constellation  Energy分
別簽署20年購電協議 (特定核電機組升級 )和15年能源供
應協議(不綁定單一電廠)。兩項協議涉及電力規模合計達
3,590MW，激勵Constellation  Energy和核能股價上漲。
3. 東芝宣布投資 600億日圓 (3.8億美元 )擴建HDD產能，衝
擊希捷 、威騰股價 。然而，大摩估計東芝擴產年化增速
30%，在市占相對較小的情況下 ，對整體供給影響不大 ，
同時與加速增長的需求相比仍有缺口。
*本頁面公司、ETF名稱僅為列示，非代表單一個股推薦

Marvell釋出樂觀營收展望
5
Marvell上調營收預期
圖表解讀
➢ 10/6 Marvell(MRVL)在其投資人日上上調2028FY營收
展 望 至200 億 美 元( 原為 180 億 美 元) ， 並 首 次 釋 出
2031FY營收目標至700~900億美元，遠超市場預期的
621億美元，激勵股價再次上漲。
➢ 根 據 財 測 指 引 換 算， Marvell  2029~2031FY 營收
CAGR增速高達 58.7%，高於2027FY預計的 47%，代
表在高基期下 ，成長不僅未放緩 ，甚至加速成長 。此
外，公司指出 2031FY營收中位數目標 800億美元是針
對「已取得的設計訂單 」所做的推估 ，代表訂單能見
度高，達成機率大。
➢ Marvell營收的樂觀預期再次為 AI資本支出、半導體產
業趨勢背書 ，科技股在前景明確下 ，依舊會是投資主
軸和資金青睞的方向。

### Page 6

巴西大選變天，股市有望複製歷史
6
波索納羅首輪總統大選位居領先 圖表解讀
➢ 10/4巴西舉行首輪總統大選 ，前總統之子弗拉維奧·波
索納羅意外以 47.1%的得票率領先現任總統魯拉 ，雙
方將於10/25進行第二輪決選。此次波索納羅的領先明
顯超出市場預期 ，在選前的平均民調上 ，波索納羅落
後2.3%，也因此在首輪取得領先後 ，巴西股市 、債市
預期新政府較容易推動財政緊縮 、企業減稅等政策而
大漲慶祝，
➢ 2010~2022年巴西四次總統大選後股市普遍走跌(90個
交易日) ，唯一上漲是2018年(雅伊爾.波索納羅勝選)，
同樣主張縮減政府開支 ，加上當時通膨降溫 ，引導巴
西殖利率走低 。目前巴西正經歷同樣的市況 ，市場預
期未來1年巴西央行仍有7碼降息空間。
➢ 目前MSCI巴西股市預期PE為9.4X，不僅低於20年平均
的10.4X，也低於過去四次總統大選前的水位 ，為後續
股市創造上漲空間。
資料來源： GS, 2026/10/4(上)、Bloomberg, 202610/7(下)

### Page 7

通膨有望持穩，官員談話或淡化升息預期
7
重點提醒
➢ 本週全球總經重點聚焦在通膨數據 ，以及FED官員談話
態度，在美債殖利率持續飆升之際 ，成為市場短期定價
的風向球。
➢ 8月美國核心 PCE維持3%的穩定增速 ，再次證明美國並
未見到通膨上升的壓力 ， 同時 9 月即時通膨指標
Truflation平均年增率 2.41%，也同樣低於 8月的2.51%，
然而通膨僵固性確實存在。9月FED剛展開升息循環，利
率上升對於物價的壓抑效果尚未體現 ，預期9月CPI仍將
維持穩定增速。
➢ Hammack 9月談話明顯鷹派 ，認為企業與家庭 或將高
物價視為常態 ，使高通膨更難消除 ，政策須維持限制性
立場。而Waller 9月談話相對鴿派 ，認為若通膨未明顯
攀升，仍傾向維持利率不變 ，在期貨市場預期未來 FED
仍有4碼的升息空間情況下，或有機會降溫市場情緒。

### Page 8

美元續強，G7釋儲油壓抑油價
8
匯市表現說明
➢ 歐元： 歐洲央行官員表態 ，法國債券走勢目前尚不足以
觸發其重啟購債計畫門檻 ，同時法國財政部表示將維持
發債策略，歐元仍承受極大壓力。
➢ 瑞朗： 相較於全球主要央行重啟升息循環 ，瑞士央行持
續將政策利率維持在 0%，在美元走強之際 ，同步引導瑞

➢ 原油： 沙國正持續透過替代管道將原由運出 ，中東原油
出口的恢復，疊加G7宣布在4個月內釋出最多一億桶柴油、
原油儲備，引導油價下跌。

金價走勢 。然而，全球央行買入黃金的趨勢並未縮減 ，
中國人行連續 23個月增持黃金 ，長期依舊看好金價動能 。

### Page 9

法國財政見不到曙光，美元維持強勢預期
9
短期美德利差或引導美元暫歇 圖表解讀
➢ 法國財政疑慮引發歐元走弱 ，同時疊加美國升息 、債
務持續性問題 ，美德利差快速飆升 ，推升美元指數走
強。然而，短期美德 10年利差上升速度過快 ，加上殖
利率已完全反應FED升息預期，美元指數升勢或獲得暫
緩空間。
➢ 然而，中長期仍看好美元維持相對強勢 。2027年4月
18號~5月2號法國將舉行總統大選 ，預期新任總統將
於6月再次解散國會改選以解決國會僵局 。但以目前賭
盤機率 ，極右派 Le Pen和中間派 Philippe分別以 33%
和26%位居前二 ，未有黨派能單獨過半的情勢仍高機
率延續至2027年。
➢ 在市場無法見到明確的政治僵局突破或財政赤字緩解
的跡象前，不確定因素將延續至 2027年，持續干擾歐
元，引導美元強勢。

### Page 10

海外資金匯回潛在買盤提供日圓支撐
10
日本殖利率並未如美債、法債反應劇烈 圖表解讀
➢ 相對於「震央核心」的法國、美國，9月日本殖利率上
升幅度明顯較低 ，在日本債務/GDP比重高於法國 、美
國的情況下 ，此舉並不尋常 ，關鍵在於市場期待規模
高達2兆美元的政府退休金投資基金 (GPIF) 有望降低海
外債券，提高國內債券持有比重。
➢ 10月日本十年公債標售倍數達 3.76X，連續3個月回升，
也證明市場對日本財政擔憂並未在此次全球財政疑慮
中爆發，一定程度上緩解日圓貶勢。
➢ 此波日圓雖未隨美元強勢而大幅貶值 ，但美元/日圓短
期仍有向上回測160價位可能，市場將再次挑戰日本政
府對於阻貶日圓的決心 ，但在基金 、保險公司等潛在
資金匯回的買盤下，預期貶破前波低點164附近的機率
不高。

### Page 11

利率高檔盤整，債市短暫獲得喘息空間
11
債市表現說明
➢ 美國公債：上週美國十年公債拍賣投標倍數達2.77倍，創
下2016年以來新高，市場對於公債需求保持穩定。在9月
殖利率飆升後 ，上週十年美債殖利率維持高檔震盪 ，也
為債市提供暫時性的喘息空間。
➢ 投等債：9月下旬後，美國投等債信用利差雖然小幅上升
約5bp，但就目前水準值80bp而言，仍遠低於過去5年平
均的102bp，美國投等債企業財務體質依舊穩健。
➢ 非投等債： 相較於投等債的穩定 ，上週全球非投等債的
信用利差上升幅度約 40bp，水準值逐漸逼近今年 3月美
伊戰爭爆發時的波段高峰，尤其CCC及以下評等更是主要
拖累關鍵 ，市場對於中小企業能否在利率攀升之際 ，維
持獲利穩定感到懷疑。
